The mayor even threw us a parade down Main Street... \n\nBut, uh, I'm sorry, what was the question again? Oh, right, thoughts jumping around. No, I wouldn't say they do that. They just follow a line.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finding the right words when trying to express your thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I usually know exactly what I want to say. The words come to me just fine. My vocabulary is pretty good, actually. I used to do the crossword puzzle in the Sunday paper every single week. My wife and I would sit at the kitchen table with our coffee—she liked hers black, but I always needed a little cream and sugar. Anyway, we'd race to see who could finish their half of the puzzle first. She was a whiz at the anagrams. She used to read all those mystery novels, Agatha Christie, Sherlock Holmes, all of them. We actually went to London once, years ago, and saw Baker Street. It rained the whole time we were there, but we didn't mind. We bought these matching umbrellas from a little shop near the British Museum... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't have trouble finding words. I can usually say what's on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to something for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. If it's something I need to do, I can stick with it. Like when I'm out in the garage working on my car. I've got this '68 Mustang I've been restoring for the better part of a decade. It's a real beauty, cherry red. I spent an entire weekend just polishing the chrome on the bumpers once. You really have to get in there with the right kind of cloth, otherwise you leave streaks. I buy these special microfiber towels from a guy at the auto parts store downtown. He's a good kid, always saves the best ones for me. His dad used to run the place before he retired and moved down to Florida. They bought a little condo right on the beach in Clearwater. I've always wanted to go to Florida, maybe see the Everglades. I watched a show about the alligators down there, how they just sit perfectly still in the water like logs waiting for a bird or something to land too close...\n\nBut, um, attention, right? Yeah, no, I don't really have trouble with that. If I'm doing something, I just do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning and organizing your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose if I have a lot on my plate on the same day, it can get a little tricky. Like, if I have a few errands to run, getting my workshop ready, and maybe an appointment... I was out in the workshop just the other day, actually. I'm building a set of cedar planter boxes for the front porch. Cedar's the best wood for that, holds up against the rot and the bugs. Smells fantastic, too. Reminds me of this old cedar chest my grandmother used to keep at the foot of her bed. She kept all these beautiful quilt blankets in there. She stitched them all by hand, every single one. Took her months just to finish one. We still have the one she made for our wedding, it's got this intricate star pattern on it, mostly blues and yellows... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, getting things done. Yeah, if I have all those things happening on the same day—the errands, the workshop, an appointment—I do have trouble putting them in the right order. I'll end up starting one thing, stopping halfway through to do something else, and then I'm just rushing around at the very end of the day trying to finish it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making decisions or figuring out what to do when faced with a complex situation?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think so. If something comes up, I just figure it out. Like when the pipes froze last winter. I went right down to the basement and shut off the main valve. You have to know where that is, you know? A lot of people don't. My neighbor, Dave, his pipes burst a few years ago and flooded his whole finished basement. He had just put down this beautiful wall-to-wall carpeting, a real nice plush beige. Ruined the whole thing. He had to rip it all out and he ended up putting down that laminate flooring that looks like wood. It's not bad, actually. He got it on sale at the big hardware store out by the highway. The one with the huge garden center. I like going there in the spring to look at the tomato plants. I always plant a few Early Girls and some Beefsteaks. There's nothing like a fresh tomato sandwich with a little mayonnaise and salt. My mother used to make those for me when I was a kid during the summer... \n\nBut, um, decisions? No, I don't really have a problem with that. I just do what needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble breaking down large tasks into smaller, manageable steps?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. If I have a big project, I just get started on it. Like when I decided to paint the exterior of the house a few years back. It's a two-story, so I had to rent one of those big extension ladders. The guy at the rental place was real helpful, showed me how to lock the rungs properly so it doesn't slide down on you while you're up there. Safety first, he kept saying. He was missing a piece of his left index finger, actually. I didn't ask how it happened, but you figure a guy working around heavy equipment all his life, something's bound to catch you eventually. My uncle Arthur lost a toe once to a lawnmower. It was one of those old heavy steel ones, not the plastic things they make today. He was wearing sandals, which was his first mistake. You never do yard work in open-toed shoes. He used to joke about it later, though, said he was lighter on his feet after that... \n\nBut, uh, breaking things down? No, I just get the ladder, get the paint, and start at the top. You always start at the top so the drips don't ruin what you've already done. I don't really have a problem with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making lists or using notes to help you remember things?\n\nPatient: Lists? No, I don't have any trouble making them. I actually make lists all the time. I keep a little notepad right by the telephone in the kitchen. It's one of those magnetic ones you stick to the fridge, you know? The one we have right now has a picture of a little cartoon chef on it. I think we got it from that Italian place down on Main Street. Luigi's, I think it's called. Best garlic knots in town, hands down. They bake them fresh every morning, and the smell just hits you the second you walk in the door. We went there for my wife's birthday last year. Or was it our anniversary? No, birthday, because it was snowing. It always snows in February here. I remember one February back during the blizzard of '78, the snow was piled up all the way to the top of the front door. We had to climb out a window just to shovel the walkway. Took us three days to dig out the driveway, and even then, the city plows came by and pushed it all right back in. Typical, right? You do all that work and then... bam. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, notes. Right. No, I don't have any trouble writing things down. I just grab a pen and put it on the fridge pad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to find the right words when speaking without preparing what you want to say ahead of time?\n\nPatient: Finding the right words? No, I wouldn't say that. I usually just say what's on my mind. I mean, my wife says I talk too much sometimes, especially when we have company over. We had the Millers over for dinner last Sunday. Nice folks. Richard Miller, he just retired from the post office after thirty-something years. He was telling me about how much the mail routes have changed since he started back in the eighties. You know, with all the email and text messaging nowadays, nobody sends letters anymore. It's mostly just packages from those online stores. He said his truck used to be full of envelopes, and by the time he retired, it was like driving a delivery van for cardboard boxes. We order dog food online now, actually. It's so much easier than lugging those forty-pound bags from the pet store to the car, and then from the car to the house. My back just can't take that kind of lifting anymore. I pulled a muscle a few years ago moving a sofa, and it's never been quite the same since. The doctor told me to use a heating pad and take it easy, but you know how it is, there's always something that needs fixing around the house. \n\nBut, uh, no, struggling with words? Not really. I just talk. I don't really need to practice or anything before I speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever struggle to find the right words when you're writing something down, like a letter or a note?\n\nPatient: Writing things down? No, I don't really have a problem with that. If I need to write a note or a letter, the words come to me just fine. I actually wrote a nice long letter to my sister last Christmas. She lives out in Arizona now. Moved there about five years ago because her husband got transferred. He works for some big aerospace company, designing parts for airplanes or something like that. I've never been out there myself, but she says the heat is a \"dry heat,\" whatever that means. Hot is hot, if you ask me. Like that summer we had back in '99, it was over ninety degrees for two weeks straight, and our central air went out on the second day. The repair guy couldn't come out for a week because everyone's AC was breaking down at the same time. We had to buy three of those cheap box fans from the hardware store just to be able to sleep at night. They were so loud, it sounded like you were sleeping on an airport runway. But you get used to the noise after a while, you know? It just kind of becomes background hum. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, writing. Right. No, I don't struggle to find words when I write. I just sit down with a pen and it comes out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty recalling what you had for breakfast this morning or what you did earlier today?\n\nPatient: Breakfast? No, I remember that fine. I had my usual—a bowl of oatmeal with a little brown sugar and some raisins. I always get those raisins in the little red boxes. They remind me of when I was a kid. My mother used to pack them in my lunchbox every single day when I went to Jefferson Elementary. That old school building isn't even there anymore, they tore it down back in... oh, it must have been '05 or '06, to build that new strip mall. The one with the big hardware store and the frozen yogurt place. I went to that yogurt place once with my grandson, Tommy. He got this bright blue flavor that looked like something you'd put in your car radiator, and he covered it in gummy worms. Kids today eat the strangest things. When I was his age, a treat was a scoop of vanilla ice cream with a little chocolate syrup if you were lucky. We used to get it from the soda fountain at Miller's Pharmacy. Old Mr. Miller, he knew everyone in town. He'd always give you a piece of hard candy when you came in to pick up a prescription. You just don't see that kind of customer service anymore. Everything is so automated now, you just scan a barcode and off you go. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, earlier today. Yeah, I remember fine. After breakfast, I just watched a little news and read the paper. No trouble remembering any of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things from one minute to the next?\n\nPatient: From one minute to the next? No, my memory's fine like that. I don't forget things right after they happen. Although, my wife did buy me one of those fancy smartwatches for my birthday last year, supposedly to help me keep track of things. It's got all these bells and whistles, tracks your steps, your heart rate... it even tells you to stand up if you've been sitting too long. I mean, I know when my legs are stiff, I don't need a computer on my wrist to tell me to take a walk. I tried wearing it for a week, but it kept buzzing every time I got an email. And the battery! You have to charge the darn thing every single night. I'm used to my old Timex. Had it for twenty years, just replace the battery every couple of years and it keeps ticking. I think I left the smartwatch in a drawer somewhere in the guest room. The guest room, we finally painted it last spring. We went with a nice pale yellow. It really brightens up the space, especially since that big oak tree in the front yard blocks most of the morning sun. We had to hire a guy to trim some of the branches back because they were scraping against the roof during that bad storm we had in April. Lost a few shingles, but nothing major. \n\nBut, uh, no, my short-term memory is okay. I don't lose track of things from minute to minute.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Something that just happened? No, I don't think so. If it just happened, it's right there in my head. Like when the mail carrier came by today. I saw him drop off the letters. He's a new guy, much younger than Frank. Frank was our mailman for... gosh, must have been fifteen years. He retired last fall, bought an RV, and drove down to Florida with his wife. They send us a postcard every now and then. The last one had an alligator on it, wearing sunglasses. I don't know why they always put sunglasses on animals in those tourist shops. It's silly. We bought him that dog at the county fair three years ago. Or was it four? No, three, because it was the year my wife won a blue ribbon for her cherry pie. She uses a secret ingredient in the crust—a little splash of vodka, if you can believe it. She says it makes it extra flaky because the alcohol evaporates in the oven. I just know it tastes good. I usually have a slice with my coffee on Sunday mornings.

My buddy Frank and I, we go down to the diner every Tuesday morning for coffee, and we talk for hours. He's got this old Ford Mustang he's been restoring for, gosh, it must be ten years now. He finally got the engine running last month. Sounded like a lawnmower at first, but he tweaked something with the carburetor and now it purrs. I told him he needs to paint it that cherry red color they used to have back in the sixties. My brother had a red one. He drove that thing all the way out to California right after he graduated. Said he was gonna be a surfer, but he ended up working in a hardware store in Fresno... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't think I have a hard time staying on topic. I usually just talk about whatever we're talking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected or hard to follow when you're talking or thinking to yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say my thoughts are disconnected. They make sense to me, anyway. I just think about normal things, you know? Like this morning, I was thinking about the weather. It's supposed to rain later, which is a shame because I was planning to go down to the hardware store. I need to get some of that special glue for a birdhouse I'm building. It's a bluebird house. You have to make the hole exactly the right size, or the starlings will get in and chase the bluebirds out. Starlings are just awful birds. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she puts out suet for them, and I keep telling her, \"Mildred, you're just encouraging the bullies!\" But she doesn't listen. She's a sweet lady, though. Makes a wonderful peach cobbler. She brought me a slice last week when I was out raking the leaves. \n\nBut yeah, my thoughts seem fine to me. They just flow along like they always have.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're forgetting things that happened recently, like what you did yesterday or the day before?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really have trouble with that. I remember what I did yesterday just fine. Yesterday was... let's see, it was Wednesday, so I went to the grocery store in the morning. I always go on Wednesdays because they have that senior discount on produce. Though, honestly, the tomatoes they've been getting lately are just pathetic. Hard as rocks and no flavor at all. You can't get a decent tomato unless you grow it yourself. I used to have a wonderful vegetable garden out back. Grew these big, beautiful Beefsteak tomatoes. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to make the most incredible homemade salsa with them. We'd sit out on the porch in the summer, eating salsa and chips and listening to the cicadas. She had this old radio out there, tuned to the classical station. She loved her Mozart. \n\nBut anyway, no, my memory for recent things is pretty sharp. I don't forget what I did yesterday or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to learn new things, like how to use a new device or remember a new procedure?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I think I pick up on new stuff just fine. Like, my daughter got me one of those new smart TVs for Christmas last year, and I figured out the remote pretty quick. It's got all these buttons for the internet on it, you know, Netflix and all that. I was watching this documentary on there the other night about the Hoover Dam. It's just incredible how they built that thing back in the thirties. The amount of concrete they used... they had to run ice water through pipes inside it just to cool it down so it would set right. My uncle Arthur actually worked on a dam out in Oregon back in the day. He was a tough old bird. Used to smoke a pipe that smelled like cherry wood. I always loved that smell. Reminds me of autumn, when the leaves start turning and you get that crispness in the air... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, learning things. Yeah, no, I don't have any trouble with that. I can figure out how things work if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that your mind jumps abruptly from one thought to a completely unrelated thought?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. They don't just jump completely out of nowhere, they... they always connect somehow, you know? Like one thing leads to the next, kind of like a train on a track. I actually used to have this massive Lionel model train setup down in my basement. Took up almost half the room. I spent years building these little towns and mountains out of papier-mâché for it to run through. It was really something, right up until the sump pump failed during that huge storm we had a few years back. We got two feet of water down there. Ruined the tracks, ruined a bunch of cardboard boxes I had sitting on the floor... lost my old high school letterman jacket in that flood. I played baseball, you see. Second base. We actually made it to the state championships my senior year, which was a huge deal for our school. The mayor even threw us a parade down Main Street... \n\nBut, uh, I'm sorry, what was the question again? Oh, right, thoughts jumping around. No, I wouldn't say they do that. They just follow a line.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finding the right words when trying to express your thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I usually know exactly what I want to say. The words come to me just fine. My vocabulary is pretty good, actually. I used to do the crossword puzzle in the Sunday paper every single week. My wife and I would sit at the kitchen table with our coffee—she liked hers black, but I always needed a little cream and sugar. Anyway, we'd race to see who could finish their half of the puzzle first. She was a whiz at the anagrams. She used to read all those mystery novels, Agatha Christie, Sherlock Holmes, all of them. We actually went to London once, years ago, and saw Baker Street. It rained the whole time we were there, but we didn't mind. We bought these matching umbrellas from a little shop near the British Museum... \n\nBut, uh, no, I don't have trouble finding words. I can usually say what's on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to something for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. If it's something I need to do, I can stick with it. Like when I'm out in the garage working on my car. I've got this '68 Mustang I've been restoring for the better part of a decade. It's a real beauty, cherry red. I spent an entire weekend just polishing the chrome on the bumpers once. You really have to get in there with the right kind of cloth, otherwise you leave streaks. I buy these special microfiber towels from a guy at the auto parts store downtown. He's a good kid, always saves the best ones for me. His dad used to run the place before he retired and moved down to Florida. They bought a little condo right on the beach in Clearwater. I've always wanted to go to Florida, maybe see the Everglades. I watched a show about the alligators down there, how they just sit perfectly still in the water like logs waiting for a bird or something to land too close...\n\nBut, um, attention, right? Yeah, no, I don't really have trouble with that. If I'm doing something, I just do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning and organizing your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose if I have a lot on my plate on the same day, it can get a little tricky. Like, if I have a few errands to run, getting my workshop ready, and maybe an appointment... I was out in the workshop just the other day, actually. I'm building a set of cedar planter boxes for the front porch. Cedar's the best wood for that, holds up against the rot and the bugs. Smells fantastic, too. Reminds me of this old cedar chest my grandmother used to keep at the foot of her bed. She kept all these beautiful quilt blankets in there. She stitched them all by hand, every single one. Took her months just to finish one. We still have the one she made for our wedding, it's got this intricate star pattern on it, mostly blues and yellows... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, getting things done. Yeah, if I have all those things happening on the same day—the errands, the workshop, an appointment—I do have trouble putting them in the right order. I'll end up starting one thing, stopping halfway through to do something else, and then I'm just rushing around at the very end of the day trying to finish it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making decisions or figuring out what to do when faced with a complex situation?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think so. If something comes up, I just figure it out. Like when the pipes froze last winter. I went right down to the basement and shut off the main valve. You have to know where that is, you know? A lot of people don't. My neighbor, Dave, his pipes burst a few years ago and flooded his whole finished basement. He had just put down this beautiful wall-to-wall carpeting, a real nice plush beige. Ruined the whole thing. He had to rip it all out and he ended up putting down that laminate flooring that looks like wood. It's not bad, actually. He got it on sale at the big hardware store out by the highway. The one with the huge garden center. I like going there in the spring to look at the tomato plants. I always plant a few Early Girls and some Beefsteaks. There's nothing like a fresh tomato sandwich with a little mayonnaise and salt. My mother used to make those for me when I was a kid during the summer... \n\nBut, um, decisions? No, I don't really have a problem with that. I just do what needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble breaking down large tasks into smaller, manageable steps?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. If I have a big project, I just get started on it. Like when I decided to paint the exterior of the house a few years back. It's a two-story, so I had to rent one of those big extension ladders. The guy at the rental place was real helpful, showed me how to lock the rungs properly so it doesn't slide down on you while you're up there. Safety first, he kept saying. He was missing a piece of his left index finger, actually. I didn't ask how it happened, but you figure a guy working around heavy equipment all his life, something's bound to catch you eventually. My uncle Arthur lost a toe once to a lawnmower. It was one of those old heavy steel ones, not the plastic things they make today. He was wearing sandals, which was his first mistake. You never do yard work in open-toed shoes. He used to joke about it later, though, said he was lighter on his feet after that... \n\nBut, uh, breaking things down? No, I just get the ladder, get the paint, and start at the top. You always start at the top so the drips don't ruin what you've already done. I don't really have a problem with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making lists or using notes to help you remember things?\n\nPatient: Lists? No, I don't have any trouble making them. I actually make lists all the time. I keep a little notepad right by the telephone in the kitchen. It's one of those magnetic ones you stick to the fridge, you know? The one we have right now has a picture of a little cartoon chef on it. I think we got it from that Italian place down on Main Street. Luigi's, I think it's called. Best garlic knots in town, hands down. They bake them fresh every morning, and the smell just hits you the second you walk in the door. We went there for my wife's birthday last year. Or was it our anniversary? No, birthday, because it was snowing. It always snows in February here. I remember one February back during the blizzard of '78, the snow was piled up all the way to the top of the front door. We had to climb out a window just to shovel the walkway. Took us three days to dig out the driveway, and even then, the city plows came by and pushed it all right back in. Typical, right? You do all that work and then... bam. \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, notes. Right. No, I don't have any trouble writing things down. I just grab a pen and put it on the fridge pad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to find the right words when speaking without preparing what you want to say ahead of time?\n\nPatient: Finding the right words? No, I wouldn't say that. I usually just say what's on my mind. I mean, my wife says I talk too much sometimes, especially when we have company over. We had the Millers over for dinner last Sunday. Nice folks. Richard Miller, he just retired from the post office after thirty-something years. He was telling me about how much the mail routes have changed since he started back in the eighties. You know, with all the email and text messaging nowadays, nobody sends letters anymore. It's mostly just packages from those online stores. He said his truck used to be full of envelopes, and by the time he retired, it was like driving a delivery van for cardboard boxes. We order dog food online now, actually. It's so much easier than lugging those forty-pound bags from the pet store to the car, and then from the car to the house. My back just can't take that kind of lifting anymore. I pulled a muscle a few years ago moving a sofa, and it's never been quite the same since. The doctor told me to use a heating pad and take it easy, but you know how it is, there's always something that needs fixing around the house. \n\nBut, uh, no, struggling with words? Not really. I just talk. I don't really need to practice or anything before I speak.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever struggle to find the right words when you're writing something down, like a letter or a note?\n\nPatient: Writing things down? No, I don't really have a problem with that. If I need to write a note or a letter, the words come to me just fine. I actually wrote a nice long letter to my sister last Christmas. She lives out in Arizona now. Moved there about five years ago because her husband got transferred. He works for some big aerospace company, designing parts for airplanes or something like that. I've never been out there myself, but she says the heat is a \"dry heat,\" whatever that means. Hot is hot, if you ask me. Like that summer we had back in '99, it was over ninety degrees for two weeks straight, and our central air went out on the second day. The repair guy couldn't come out for a week because everyone's AC was breaking down at the same time. We had to buy three of those cheap box fans from the hardware store just to be able to sleep at night. They were so loud, it sounded like you were sleeping on an airport runway. But you get used to the noise after a while, you know? It just kind of becomes background hum. \n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, writing. Right. No, I don't struggle to find words when I write. I just sit down with a pen and it comes out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty recalling what you had for breakfast this morning or what you did earlier today?\n\nPatient: Breakfast? No, I remember that fine. I had my usual—a bowl of oatmeal with a little brown sugar and some raisins. I always get those raisins in the little red boxes. They remind me of when I was a kid. My mother used to pack them in my lunchbox every single day when I went to Jefferson Elementary. That old school building isn't even there anymore, they tore it down back in... oh, it must have been '05 or '06, to build that new strip mall. The one with the big hardware store and the frozen yogurt place. I went to that yogurt place once with my grandson, Tommy. He got this bright blue flavor that looked like something you'd put in your car radiator, and he covered it in gummy worms. Kids today eat the strangest things. When I was his age, a treat was a scoop of vanilla ice cream with a little chocolate syrup if you were lucky. We used to get it from the soda fountain at Miller's Pharmacy. Old Mr. Miller, he knew everyone in town. He'd always give you a piece of hard candy when you came in to pick up a prescription. You just don't see that kind of customer service anymore. Everything is so automated now, you just scan a barcode and off you go. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, earlier today. Yeah, I remember fine. After breakfast, I just watched a little news and read the paper. No trouble remembering any of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things from one minute to the next?\n\nPatient: From one minute to the next? No, my memory's fine like that. I don't forget things right after they happen. Although, my wife did buy me one of those fancy smartwatches for my birthday last year, supposedly to help me keep track of things. It's got all these bells and whistles, tracks your steps, your heart rate... it even tells you to stand up if you've been sitting too long. I mean, I know when my legs are stiff, I don't need a computer on my wrist to tell me to take a walk. I tried wearing it for a week, but it kept buzzing every time I got an email. And the battery! You have to charge the darn thing every single night. I'm used to my old Timex. Had it for twenty years, just replace the battery every couple of years and it keeps ticking. I think I left the smartwatch in a drawer somewhere in the guest room. The guest room, we finally painted it last spring. We went with a nice pale yellow. It really brightens up the space, especially since that big oak tree in the front yard blocks most of the morning sun. We had to hire a guy to trim some of the branches back because they were scraping against the roof during that bad storm we had in April. Lost a few shingles, but nothing major. \n\nBut, uh, no, my short-term memory is okay. I don't lose track of things from minute to minute.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unable to recall something that just happened?\n\nPatient: Something that just happened? No, I don't think so. If it just happened, it's right there in my head. Like when the mail carrier came by today. I saw him drop off the letters. He's a new guy, much younger than Frank. Frank was our mailman for... gosh, must have been fifteen years. He retired last fall, bought an RV, and drove down to Florida with his wife. They send us a postcard every now and then. The last one had an alligator on it, wearing sunglasses. I don't know why they always put sunglasses on animals in those tourist shops. It's silly. My grandson Tommy thinks it's hilarious, though. He's got this stuffed dog he put a pair of my old reading glasses on. Kept calling it \"Professor Bark.\" Kids have such imaginations. We bought him that dog at the county fair three years ago. Or was it four? No, three, because it was the year my wife won a blue ribbon for her cherry pie. She uses a secret ingredient in the crust—a little splash of vodka, if you can believe it. She says it makes it extra flaky because the alcohol evaporates in the oven. I just know it tastes good. I usually have a slice with my coffee on Sunday mornings. \n\nBut anyway... what were you asking? Oh, if I forget things that just happened. No, no, I'm pretty sharp with that kind of stuff. If it just happened, I know it happened."
